I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my husband is the one who wanted me to come talk to you, but honestly, I am just at my wits' end with this whole nightmare and I just need someone to listen. It started, oh, it has to be nearly two years ago now. We hired a contractor for the house, and you know how that goes, you expect a little mess, but this... this is entirely different. \n\nFor two years, I have been absolutely sure, because I have eyes and I can see what's happening, that this contractor and some of the people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. The cabinets don't line up, the fixtures are left unfinished, the tiles are just sitting there, and it is not an accident. I see the contractor chatting with the neighbors at the end of the driveway, whispering, and then suddenly there's another delay. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he just doesn't get it. He keeps digging into his filing cabinet and bringing out all these papers and he shows me the receipts over and over, trying to prove that everything is above board and that we only paid for what was done. And the contractor and his men, they give us so many excuses about supply chains and backorders and honest mistakes. But you don't make the same mistakes in a kitchen for two whole years unless you are doing it on purpose to cheat someone, and they are all in on it together, and I just can't get anyone in my own house to see what is going on right under our noses!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were being targeted or singled out for special reasons, or that you had a special role or importance in what was happening with the contractor and your neighbors?\n\nPatient: Special? Oh, heavens no, doctor, I’m not the Queen of England or a secret agent or anything silly like that. I’m just a normal woman who wants to be able to use her own stove! We’ve lived in that house on Maple Drive for thirty-two years. I worked in the school cafeteria for most of my life, my husband was at the post office... there’s absolutely nothing special about us. We don't have a fortune buried in the backyard or anything they'd be after. \n\nIt’s just... well, it’s just plain, ugly greed, if you ask me. I think this contractor—his name is Gary, by the way, though I wouldn't recommend him to my worst enemy—I think he just looked at us, saw an older, retired couple, and thought, 'Here’s an easy mark.' And as for the neighbors, well, they've always been a bit nosy anyway. Linda next door, she’s always out there with her little yappy terrier, watching everyone’s business. I think Gary just roped them in because they like the drama and they like seeing us struggle. They stand by the fence, whispering, and I can see Linda nodding while Gary points at my driveway. \n\nBut no, there’s no grand reason because of who I am. I don't have any special role in the world. I’m just a homeowner trying to get what we paid for. And it is so incredibly frustrating because Gary will come up to the porch with his clipboard, and he’ll have this little smirk, and he’ll say, 'Oh, the cabinet hinges are on backorder from overseas,' but I know for a fact you can buy them right down at the local hardware store on 4th Street because I drove down there and looked myself! I stood right in aisle six and saw them hanging there. So why would he tell me they're stuck on a boat somewhere unless he's just trying to milk us dry and keep the job going? It's not because I'm important, doctor, it's just because they think I'm stupid. And I'm not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something strongly that others didn't, or saw or heard things that others couldn't, outside of what's been happening with the contractor and your neighbors?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my goodness, I'm not crazy, doctor. I don't go around seeing ghosts or hearing voices in the walls or anything out of a movie. I've always been a very grounded person. When I worked at the school, you had to be grounded, you know? You can't be off in the clouds when you have three hundred children coming through the lunch line expecting their sloppy joes and tater tots exactly at 11:30. I was always the one making sure the inventory was right, making sure the milk wasn't expired. I deal in facts. I deal in what is right in front of me.\n\nAnd I've never had any, what do you call them, visions or wild ideas about anything else. It's just this one thing, this one horrible, unending nightmare with the kitchen. Because it's real! If someone else came and stood in my kitchen and looked at the grout—or the lack of grout, I should say—and saw the way Gary looks at me when he hands me another revised invoice, they would see it too. But as for anything else? No. My life was perfectly quiet and perfectly normal before we decided to update those awful 1980s oak cabinets. I sometimes wish we had just kept them, to be honest. At least they had doors that closed properly, not like the mess we have now, where half the hinges are missing and the other half are crooked. But no, I don't hear things or see things that aren't there. I just see what Gary is doing, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that others found strange or hard to understand, even if you were certain they were true at the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, strange beliefs? No, no, I really haven't. I mean, my husband might say I have strong opinions on things, like how I absolutely refuse to buy anything but the store brand paper towels because the expensive ones just smear the dirt around—I've tested it, doctor, I used them side-by-side on the living room windows back in 2015, and the store brand was far superior—but that’s not what you mean, is it? You mean strange things, like thinking the government is reading my mail or that the moon landing was faked. \n\nNo, I’ve never been one for nonsense. Like I said, I’m a practical woman. I spent thirty years serving lunch to middle schoolers, you don’t have time for strange beliefs when you’re trying to keep track of who paid for extra milk and who didn't. I’ve always just seen the world exactly as it is. If it's raining, I take an umbrella. If the car makes a funny noise, I take it to the mechanic on Elm Street—though he retired last year, which is a shame, because his son took over and he charges an arm and a leg just to look under the hood. \n\nBut as for things people find hard to understand... well, the only thing is this whole situation with Gary and the neighbors. That’s the only time in my entire life where people have looked at me like I’m speaking a foreign language. And it is so baffling to me! Because it’s not a strange belief, it’s just facts. It’s right there in the unfinished crown molding that he promised would be done by Thanksgiving of last year. But before this? Never. I was just a regular lady who liked her garden and her cross-stitch and keeping a tidy house, which I can't even do now with all this dust everywhere! So no, doctor, no strange beliefs at all. Just the truth about what’s happening in my own home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you believed something was definitely true, even though people close to you, like your husband or friends, told you it wasn’t, and later you realized they were right?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, doctor. I've never had a situation like that. Honestly, my husband, bless his heart, he's a wonderful man, but he's always been a bit... well, oblivious, I suppose you could say. He worked at the post office sorting mail for thirty-five years, and he just likes things quiet. He doesn't like to make a fuss. So even when things are staring him right in the face, he’ll try to brush it off and say, 'Oh, Mary, let it go, it's fine.' \n\nLike back in 1998, when we bought that used Buick. I told him, I said, 'Frank, the transmission is slipping, I can feel it when I go up the hill on Elm Street.' And he said, 'No, no, you're just not used to an automatic, it's fine.' Well, wouldn't you know it, three months later, the whole thing gave out while I was on my way to the grocery store! I had to pull over right in front of the Methodist church and walk two miles home in the heat. So, no, I don't have times where I realize later that they were right and I was wrong. If anything, it's usually the other way around. \n\nI just pay attention to things, doctor. I notice details. And that's why this whole thing with Gary and the neighbors is so maddening. Frank will say, 'Mary, maybe Gary just forgot to order the tile,' or 'Mary, maybe Linda is just out walking her dog.' But I know what I see! I see the way they look at each other. I see Gary's truck parked two streets over sometimes, just sitting there. Why would he park two streets over if he's working on our house? Frank says it's because there's no shade on our street, but that's just him making excuses because he doesn't want to confront anybody. He hates conflict. But I won't just sit by and let people take advantage of us. I won't do it. So, no, I've never had a time where I was wrong about something important like that. I trust my own eyes, and my eyes haven't steered me wrong yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any strong beliefs that turned out not to be true, even though you were absolutely sure at the time, and this was not related to Gary or the neighbors?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... well, I feel like we're going in a bit of a circle here, because you keep asking me if I make things up or if I get confused about what's real, and the answer is just no. I don't. I've never had a time where I was so sure of something and then, oh, surprise, I was completely wrong. That's just not who I am. I'm a very careful person. I don't jump to conclusions without the evidence right there in my hands. \n\nLike I was saying about the school cafeteria—if I suspected one of our suppliers was shorting us, say, on the frozen green beans, I didn't just call them up and start making wild accusations. No, I would sit down in my little office—which was right next to the walk-in freezer, mind you, so it was always absolutely freezing in there, I had to keep a thick wool cardigan on the back of my chair even in May—and I would go through every single delivery slip for the entire month. And I would take the scale out and weigh the bags myself. And wouldn't you know it, I was right! They were sending us five-pound bags instead of the ten-pounders we paid for. I had the proof. \n\nSo I don't just get these... these notions in my head that turn out to be false. I don't have an overactive imagination at all. I don't even read fiction books, doctor, I only read biographies because I like facts. I read a very good one about Eleanor Roosevelt just last month, actually, from the library, though the print was awfully small and I had to use my reading glasses the whole time. But my point is, I deal in reality. Always have. So no, I've never been absolutely sure of something only to find out it wasn't true. I just make sure I have all my ducks in a row before I speak up about anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or trouble sleeping, that seem to get worse when you're thinking about what's happening with Gary and the neighbors?\n\nPatient: Well, physical symptoms? No... no, not really. I mean, I don't get headaches. I've never been prone to them, thank the Lord. My sister Susan, now she used to get terrible migraines back in the eighties, she'd have to lie down in a dark room with a cold washcloth over her eyes for hours, sometimes the whole afternoon, couldn't even stand the sound of the television being on downstairs. But me? No. I drink my eight glasses of water a day, just room temperature, because ice water is bad for your digestion, and I never get headaches. \n\nAnd my stomach is perfectly fine. Like I told you, I worked in a school cafeteria for thirty years, you develop an iron stomach. Plus, I eat very sensibly. Frank and I have oatmeal every single morning at 7:15, with just a little sprinkle of raisins, none of that sugary instant stuff in the little paper packets, just the plain rolled oats that I cook on the stove. So my digestion is as regular as a clock. I certainly don't get stomach aches from stress or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for sleeping, well, I sleep just fine, doctor. I turn the lights out at ten o'clock right after the local news—we watch Channel 4 because Frank likes the weatherman, though I think he talks much too fast—and I sleep straight through until six in the morning. Sometimes Frank's snoring wakes me up, he sounds like an absolute freight train when he rolls onto his back, but I just give him a little nudge in the ribs and I'm right back to sleep. \n\nSo no, I don't feel sick over this whole business with Gary and Linda. It doesn't give me physical problems. It just makes me... well, it makes me downright furious! Because it's the principle of the thing. You hire a man to do a job, you expect him to do it, not to spend his afternoons parked two streets over doing Lord knows what with the woman from next door while my house sits there in shambles. So I'm angry, yes, and I'm incredibly frustrated by all the drywall dust settling on my good china in the dining room, but I'm perfectly healthy. I don't let them make me sick. I'm far too practical to let their nonsense ruin my health.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that others might not see things the way you do because they don't interpret the same events as you do?\n\nPatient: Interpret things differently? Well... I suppose people can interpret things however they like, but facts are facts, doctor. Frank certainly tries to interpret it all as just normal construction delays because he doesn't want to make a fuss. But that is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for nearly two years now—two whole years this coming October, if you can believe it, we started tearing up the old linoleum right after Halloween—I have been absolutely sure that the contractor and some of the people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nThey are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! And I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense. The upper cabinets on the east wall don't even align with the lowers, the grout on the backsplash is completely uneven, and the island is still missing the proper base molding so you can see right underneath it to the bare floorboards. \n\nAnd Frank, bless his heart, he will go into the den and dig through his little green plastic accordion folder, and he’ll pull out all these papers. He shows me receipt after receipt, saying, 'Look Mary, here's the invoice for the lumber, here's the slip for the tile.' And Gary and Linda will give us excuse after excuse about supply chain issues or backorders from some warehouse in Ohio or whatever lie they've cooked up for the week. But it doesn't matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give. I know what they are doing. I know they are quietly colluding to drag this out and cheat us. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's a matter of people just interpreting events differently. I think it's a matter of me being the only one willing to open my eyes and look at what is actually happening, while everyone else is either in on it or letting themselves be taken for a ride.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that others don’t see things the way you do. Even though they don’t agree with you, do you believe—without any doubt—that your interpretation of what Gary and the neighbors are doing is completely and absolutely true?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, absolutely without a single doubt. I just make sure I have all my ducks in a row before I speak up about anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or trouble sleeping, that seem to get worse when you're thinking about what's happening with Gary and the neighbors?\n\nPatient: Well, physical symptoms? No... no, not really. I mean, I don't get headaches. I've never been prone to them, thank the Lord. My sister Susan, now she used to get terrible migraines back in the eighties, she'd have to lie down in a dark room with a cold washcloth over her eyes for hours, sometimes the whole afternoon, couldn't even stand the sound of the television being on downstairs. But me? No. I drink my eight glasses of water a day, just room temperature, because ice water is bad for your digestion, and I never get headaches. \n\nAnd my stomach is perfectly fine. Like I told you, I worked in a school cafeteria for thirty years, you develop an iron stomach. Plus, I eat very sensibly. Frank and I have oatmeal every single morning at 7:15, with just a little sprinkle of raisins, none of that sugary instant stuff in the little paper packets, just the plain rolled oats that I cook on the stove. So my digestion is as regular as a clock. I certainly don't get stomach aches from stress or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for sleeping, well, I sleep just fine, doctor. I turn the lights out at ten o'clock right after the local news—we watch Channel 4 because Frank likes the weatherman, though I think he talks much too fast—and I sleep straight through until six in the morning. Sometimes Frank's snoring wakes me up, he sounds like an absolute freight train when he rolls onto his back, but I just give him a little nudge in the ribs and I'm right back to sleep. \n\nSo no, I don't feel sick over this whole business with Gary and Linda. It doesn't give me physical problems. It just makes me... well, it makes me downright furious! Because it's the principle of the thing. You hire a man to do a job, you expect him to do it, not to spend his afternoons parked two streets over doing Lord knows what with the woman from next door while my house sits there in shambles. So I'm angry, yes, and I'm incredibly frustrated by all the drywall dust settling on my good china in the dining room, but I'm perfectly healthy. I don't let them make me sick. I'm far too practical to let their nonsense ruin my health.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that others might not see things the way you do because they don't interpret the same events as you do?\n\nPatient: Interpret things differently? Well... I suppose people can interpret things however they like, but facts are facts, doctor. Frank certainly tries to interpret it all as just normal construction delays because he doesn't want to make a fuss. But that is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for nearly two years now—two whole years this coming October, if you can believe it, we started tearing up the old linoleum right after Halloween—I have been absolutely sure that the contractor and some of the people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nThey are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! And I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense. The upper cabinets on the east wall don't even align with the lowers, the grout on the backsplash is completely uneven, and the island is still missing the proper base molding so you can see right underneath it to the bare floorboards. \n\nAnd Frank, bless his heart, he will go into the den and dig through his little green plastic accordion folder, and he’ll pull out all these papers. He shows me receipt after receipt, saying, 'Look Mary, here's the invoice for the lumber, here's the slip for the tile.' And Gary and Linda will give us excuse after excuse about supply chain issues or backorders from some warehouse in Ohio or whatever lie they've cooked up for the week. But it doesn't matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give. I know what they are doing. I know they are quietly colluding to drag this out and cheat us. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's a matter of people just interpreting events differently. I think it's a matter of me being the only one willing to open my eyes and look at what is actually happening, while everyone else is either in on it or letting themselves be taken for a ride.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that others don’t see things the way you do. Even though they don’t agree with you, do you believe—without any doubt—that your interpretation of what Gary and the neighbors are doing is completely and absolutely true?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, absolutely without a single doubt. Like I was just trying to explain to you a moment ago, that is the entire reason I am sitting here in this chair today. Because for nearly two years now, I have been completely, one-hundred-percent sure that the contractor and some of those people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nAnd they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! It is deliberate. You don't just 'accidentally' leave the plumbing for the dishwasher capped off for eighteen months so a sixty-seven-year-old woman has to haul her dirty plates down to the utility sink in the basement every single night. I get so tired going up and down those wooden stairs, Frank had to put down non-slip treads just last spring because I nearly took a tumble carrying a heavy stack of our good soup bowls. \n\nSo when you ask if I have any doubt... no, I do not. It does not matter one bit how many of those little faded thermal receipts my husband shows me from his green folder, trying to justify where the money went. And it doesn't matter how many excuses Gary and Linda give us about shipping delays from the manufacturer, or missing brackets, or whatever ridiculous story they've decided to spin for the week. I don't care about their excuses, and I don't care about Frank's paperwork. None of it changes the facts. I know exactly what they are doing to us, and my interpretation, as you call it, is the absolute truth. There is simply no other explanation, and I won't let anyone tell me otherwise.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I feel like a broken record here, I really do. We just went over this, didn't we? No, I do not think I am wrong, not even a fraction of a percent. I mean, I am sitting here in this office for this exact reason, like I keep trying to make you understand. For nearly two years now, I have been absolutely certain that the contractor and those people on my street are quietly working together to cheat Frank and me. And they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! 

You ask if I could be wrong, but how on earth could I be? I am not a foolish woman. I ran a cafeteria for three decades, I managed food orders, I know when inventory doesn't add up and I certainly know when people are pulling the wool over my eyes. Frank, bless him, he just doesn't want any conflict in his retirement. He will sit there in his brown leather recliner, pushing his reading glasses up his nose, and he will pull out yet another faded receipt from that green plastic folder of his and try to point out the dates for the lumber delivery or the plumbing fixtures. And then Gary will stand on my front porch with his little clipboard, wiping his forehead like he has actually been doing a hard day's work, and give me another endless string of excuses about the cabinet hardware being lost in transit or Linda needing him to look at a leak in her basement. 

But it simply doesn't matter how many little slips of paper my husband shows me. And it doesn't matter how many elaborate excuses Gary and the neighbors give. I am not wrong. They are deliberately cheating us, and they are keeping my kitchen in a state of complete disaster on purpose. So no, doctor, I haven't ever thought my understanding of the situation was wrong, because the proof is right there in my house.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my husband is the one who wanted me to come talk to you, but honestly, I am just at my wits' end with this whole nightmare and I just need someone to listen. It started, oh, it has to be nearly two years ago now. We hired a contractor for the house, and you know how that goes, you expect a little mess, but this... this is entirely different. \n\nFor two years, I have been absolutely sure, because I have eyes and I can see what's happening, that this contractor and some of the people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose. The cabinets don't line up, the fixtures are left unfinished, the tiles are just sitting there, and it is not an accident. I see the contractor chatting with the neighbors at the end of the driveway, whispering, and then suddenly there's another delay. \n\nAnd my husband, bless his heart, he just doesn't get it. He keeps digging into his filing cabinet and bringing out all these papers and he shows me the receipts over and over, trying to prove that everything is above board and that we only paid for what was done. And the contractor and his men, they give us so many excuses about supply chains and backorders and honest mistakes. But you don't make the same mistakes in a kitchen for two whole years unless you are doing it on purpose to cheat someone, and they are all in on it together, and I just can't get anyone in my own house to see what is going on right under our noses!\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that you were being targeted or singled out for special reasons, or that you had a special role or importance in what was happening with the contractor and your neighbors?\n\nPatient: Special? Oh, heavens no, doctor, I’m not the Queen of England or a secret agent or anything silly like that. I’m just a normal woman who wants to be able to use her own stove! We’ve lived in that house on Maple Drive for thirty-two years. I worked in the school cafeteria for most of my life, my husband was at the post office... there’s absolutely nothing special about us. We don't have a fortune buried in the backyard or anything they'd be after. \n\nIt’s just... well, it’s just plain, ugly greed, if you ask me. I think this contractor—his name is Gary, by the way, though I wouldn't recommend him to my worst enemy—I think he just looked at us, saw an older, retired couple, and thought, 'Here’s an easy mark.' And as for the neighbors, well, they've always been a bit nosy anyway. Linda next door, she’s always out there with her little yappy terrier, watching everyone’s business. I think Gary just roped them in because they like the drama and they like seeing us struggle. They stand by the fence, whispering, and I can see Linda nodding while Gary points at my driveway. \n\nBut no, there’s no grand reason because of who I am. I don't have any special role in the world. I’m just a homeowner trying to get what we paid for. And it is so incredibly frustrating because Gary will come up to the porch with his clipboard, and he’ll have this little smirk, and he’ll say, 'Oh, the cabinet hinges are on backorder from overseas,' but I know for a fact you can buy them right down at the local hardware store on 4th Street because I drove down there and looked myself! I stood right in aisle six and saw them hanging there. So why would he tell me they're stuck on a boat somewhere unless he's just trying to milk us dry and keep the job going? It's not because I'm important, doctor, it's just because they think I'm stupid. And I'm not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something strongly that others didn't, or saw or heard things that others couldn't, outside of what's been happening with the contractor and your neighbors?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my goodness, I'm not crazy, doctor. I don't go around seeing ghosts or hearing voices in the walls or anything out of a movie. I've always been a very grounded person. When I worked at the school, you had to be grounded, you know? You can't be off in the clouds when you have three hundred children coming through the lunch line expecting their sloppy joes and tater tots exactly at 11:30. I was always the one making sure the inventory was right, making sure the milk wasn't expired. I deal in facts. I deal in what is right in front of me.\n\nAnd I've never had any, what do you call them, visions or wild ideas about anything else. It's just this one thing, this one horrible, unending nightmare with the kitchen. Because it's real! If someone else came and stood in my kitchen and looked at the grout—or the lack of grout, I should say—and saw the way Gary looks at me when he hands me another revised invoice, they would see it too. But as for anything else? No. My life was perfectly quiet and perfectly normal before we decided to update those awful 1980s oak cabinets. I sometimes wish we had just kept them, to be honest. At least they had doors that closed properly, not like the mess we have now, where half the hinges are missing and the other half are crooked. But no, I don't hear things or see things that aren't there. I just see what Gary is doing, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any beliefs that others found strange or hard to understand, even if you were certain they were true at the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, strange beliefs? No, no, I really haven't. I mean, my husband might say I have strong opinions on things, like how I absolutely refuse to buy anything but the store brand paper towels because the expensive ones just smear the dirt around—I've tested it, doctor, I used them side-by-side on the living room windows back in 2015, and the store brand was far superior—but that’s not what you mean, is it? You mean strange things, like thinking the government is reading my mail or that the moon landing was faked. \n\nNo, I’ve never been one for nonsense. Like I said, I’m a practical woman. I spent thirty years serving lunch to middle schoolers, you don’t have time for strange beliefs when you’re trying to keep track of who paid for extra milk and who didn't. I’ve always just seen the world exactly as it is. If it's raining, I take an umbrella. If the car makes a funny noise, I take it to the mechanic on Elm Street—though he retired last year, which is a shame, because his son took over and he charges an arm and a leg just to look under the hood. \n\nBut as for things people find hard to understand... well, the only thing is this whole situation with Gary and the neighbors. That’s the only time in my entire life where people have looked at me like I’m speaking a foreign language. And it is so baffling to me! Because it’s not a strange belief, it’s just facts. It’s right there in the unfinished crown molding that he promised would be done by Thanksgiving of last year. But before this? Never. I was just a regular lady who liked her garden and her cross-stitch and keeping a tidy house, which I can't even do now with all this dust everywhere! So no, doctor, no strange beliefs at all. Just the truth about what’s happening in my own home.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you believed something was definitely true, even though people close to you, like your husband or friends, told you it wasn’t, and later you realized they were right?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, doctor. I've never had a situation like that. Honestly, my husband, bless his heart, he's a wonderful man, but he's always been a bit... well, oblivious, I suppose you could say. He worked at the post office sorting mail for thirty-five years, and he just likes things quiet. He doesn't like to make a fuss. So even when things are staring him right in the face, he’ll try to brush it off and say, 'Oh, Mary, let it go, it's fine.' \n\nLike back in 1998, when we bought that used Buick. I told him, I said, 'Frank, the transmission is slipping, I can feel it when I go up the hill on Elm Street.' And he said, 'No, no, you're just not used to an automatic, it's fine.' Well, wouldn't you know it, three months later, the whole thing gave out while I was on my way to the grocery store! I had to pull over right in front of the Methodist church and walk two miles home in the heat. So, no, I don't have times where I realize later that they were right and I was wrong. If anything, it's usually the other way around. \n\nI just pay attention to things, doctor. I notice details. And that's why this whole thing with Gary and the neighbors is so maddening. Frank will say, 'Mary, maybe Gary just forgot to order the tile,' or 'Mary, maybe Linda is just out walking her dog.' But I know what I see! I see the way they look at each other. I see Gary's truck parked two streets over sometimes, just sitting there. Why would he park two streets over if he's working on our house? Frank says it's because there's no shade on our street, but that's just him making excuses because he doesn't want to confront anybody. He hates conflict. But I won't just sit by and let people take advantage of us. I won't do it. So, no, I've never had a time where I was wrong about something important like that. I trust my own eyes, and my eyes haven't steered me wrong yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any strong beliefs that turned out not to be true, even though you were absolutely sure at the time, and this was not related to Gary or the neighbors?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... well, I feel like we're going in a bit of a circle here, because you keep asking me if I make things up or if I get confused about what's real, and the answer is just no. I don't. I've never had a time where I was so sure of something and then, oh, surprise, I was completely wrong. That's just not who I am. I'm a very careful person. I don't jump to conclusions without the evidence right there in my hands. \n\nLike I was saying about the school cafeteria—if I suspected one of our suppliers was shorting us, say, on the frozen green beans, I didn't just call them up and start making wild accusations. No, I would sit down in my little office—which was right next to the walk-in freezer, mind you, so it was always absolutely freezing in there, I had to keep a thick wool cardigan on the back of my chair even in May—and I would go through every single delivery slip for the entire month. And I would take the scale out and weigh the bags myself. And wouldn't you know it, I was right! They were sending us five-pound bags instead of the ten-pounders we paid for. I had the proof. \n\nSo I don't just get these... these notions in my head that turn out to be false. I don't have an overactive imagination at all. I don't even read fiction books, doctor, I only read biographies because I like facts. I read a very good one about Eleanor Roosevelt just last month, actually, from the library, though the print was awfully small and I had to use my reading glasses the whole time. But my point is, I deal in reality. Always have. So no, I've never been absolutely sure of something only to find out it wasn't true. I just make sure I have all my ducks in a row before I speak up about anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like headaches, stomach issues, or trouble sleeping, that seem to get worse when you're thinking about what's happening with Gary and the neighbors?\n\nPatient: Well, physical symptoms? No... no, not really. I mean, I don't get headaches. I've never been prone to them, thank the Lord. My sister Susan, now she used to get terrible migraines back in the eighties, she'd have to lie down in a dark room with a cold washcloth over her eyes for hours, sometimes the whole afternoon, couldn't even stand the sound of the television being on downstairs. But me? No. I drink my eight glasses of water a day, just room temperature, because ice water is bad for your digestion, and I never get headaches. \n\nAnd my stomach is perfectly fine. Like I told you, I worked in a school cafeteria for thirty years, you develop an iron stomach. Plus, I eat very sensibly. Frank and I have oatmeal every single morning at 7:15, with just a little sprinkle of raisins, none of that sugary instant stuff in the little paper packets, just the plain rolled oats that I cook on the stove. So my digestion is as regular as a clock. I certainly don't get stomach aches from stress or anything like that. \n\nAnd as for sleeping, well, I sleep just fine, doctor. I turn the lights out at ten o'clock right after the local news—we watch Channel 4 because Frank likes the weatherman, though I think he talks much too fast—and I sleep straight through until six in the morning. Sometimes Frank's snoring wakes me up, he sounds like an absolute freight train when he rolls onto his back, but I just give him a little nudge in the ribs and I'm right back to sleep. \n\nSo no, I don't feel sick over this whole business with Gary and Linda. It doesn't give me physical problems. It just makes me... well, it makes me downright furious! Because it's the principle of the thing. You hire a man to do a job, you expect him to do it, not to spend his afternoons parked two streets over doing Lord knows what with the woman from next door while my house sits there in shambles. So I'm angry, yes, and I'm incredibly frustrated by all the drywall dust settling on my good china in the dining room, but I'm perfectly healthy. I don't let them make me sick. I'm far too practical to let their nonsense ruin my health.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered that others might not see things the way you do because they don't interpret the same events as you do?\n\nPatient: Interpret things differently? Well... I suppose people can interpret things however they like, but facts are facts, doctor. Frank certainly tries to interpret it all as just normal construction delays because he doesn't want to make a fuss. But that is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for nearly two years now—two whole years this coming October, if you can believe it, we started tearing up the old linoleum right after Halloween—I have been absolutely sure that the contractor and some of the people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nThey are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! And I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense. The upper cabinets on the east wall don't even align with the lowers, the grout on the backsplash is completely uneven, and the island is still missing the proper base molding so you can see right underneath it to the bare floorboards. \n\nAnd Frank, bless his heart, he will go into the den and dig through his little green plastic accordion folder, and he’ll pull out all these papers. He shows me receipt after receipt, saying, 'Look Mary, here's the invoice for the lumber, here's the slip for the tile.' And Gary and Linda will give us excuse after excuse about supply chain issues or backorders from some warehouse in Ohio or whatever lie they've cooked up for the week. But it doesn't matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give. I know what they are doing. I know they are quietly colluding to drag this out and cheat us. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's a matter of people just interpreting events differently. I think it's a matter of me being the only one willing to open my eyes and look at what is actually happening, while everyone else is either in on it or letting themselves be taken for a ride.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that others don’t see things the way you do. Even though they don’t agree with you, do you believe—without any doubt—that your interpretation of what Gary and the neighbors are doing is completely and absolutely true?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, absolutely without a single doubt. Like I was just trying to explain to you a moment ago, that is the entire reason I am sitting here in this chair today. Because for nearly two years now, I have been completely, one-hundred-percent sure that the contractor and some of those people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nAnd they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! It is deliberate. You don't just 'accidentally' leave the plumbing for the dishwasher capped off for eighteen months so a sixty-seven-year-old woman has to haul her dirty plates down to the utility sink in the basement every single night. I get so tired going up and down those wooden stairs, Frank had to put down non-slip treads just last spring because I nearly took a tumble carrying a heavy stack of our good soup bowls. \n\nSo when you ask if I have any doubt... no, I do not. It does not matter one bit how many of those little faded thermal receipts my husband shows me from his green folder, trying to justify where the money went. And it doesn't matter how many excuses Gary and Linda give us about shipping delays from the manufacturer, or missing brackets, or whatever ridiculous story they've decided to spin for the week. I don't care about their excuses, and I don't care about Frank's paperwork. None of it changes the facts. I know exactly what they are doing to us, and my interpretation, as you call it, is the absolute truth. There is simply no other explanation, and I won't let anyone tell me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that maybe your understanding of what’s happening with Gary and the neighbors could be wrong, even slightly?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like a broken record here, I really do. We just went over this, didn't we? No, I do not think I am wrong, not even a fraction of a percent. I mean, I am sitting here in this office for this exact reason, like I keep trying to make you understand. For nearly two years now, I have been absolutely certain that the contractor and those people on my street are quietly working together to cheat Frank and me. And they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! \n\nYou ask if I could be wrong, but how on earth could I be? I am not a foolish woman. I ran a cafeteria for three decades, I managed food orders, I know when inventory doesn't add up and I certainly know when people are pulling the wool over my eyes. Frank, bless him, he just doesn't want any conflict in his retirement. He will sit there in his brown leather recliner, pushing his reading glasses up his nose, and he will pull out yet another faded receipt from that green plastic folder of his and try to point out the dates for the lumber delivery or the plumbing fixtures. And then Gary will stand on my front porch with his little clipboard, wiping his forehead like he has actually been doing a hard day's work, and give me another endless string of excuses about the cabinet hardware being lost in transit or Linda needing him to look at a leak in her basement. \n\nBut it simply doesn't matter how many little slips of paper my husband shows me. And it doesn't matter how many elaborate excuses Gary and the neighbors give. I am not wrong. They are deliberately cheating us, and they are keeping my kitchen in a state of complete disaster on purpose. So no, doctor, I haven't ever thought my understanding of the situation was wrong, because the proof is right there in my house.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well... I suppose people can interpret things however they like, but facts are facts, doctor. Frank certainly tries to interpret it all as just normal construction delays because he doesn't want to make a fuss. But that is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for nearly two years now—two whole years this coming October, if you can believe it, we started tearing up the old linoleum right after Halloween—I have been absolutely sure that the contractor and some of the people on my block have been quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nThey are doing it on purpose. They are deliberately keeping my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! And I mean half-wrong in the most literal sense. The upper cabinets on the east wall don't even align with the lowers, the grout on the backsplash is completely uneven, and the island is still missing the proper base molding so you can see right underneath it to the bare floorboards. \n\nAnd Frank, bless his heart, he will go into the den and dig through his little green plastic accordion folder, and he’ll pull out all these papers. He shows me receipt after receipt, saying, 'Look Mary, here's the invoice for the lumber, here's the slip for the tile.' And Gary and Linda will give us excuse after excuse about supply chain issues or backorders from some warehouse in Ohio or whatever lie they've cooked up for the week. But it doesn't matter how many receipts my husband shows me, or how many excuses they give. I know what they are doing. I know they are quietly colluding to drag this out and cheat us. \n\nSo, no, I don't think it's a matter of people just interpreting events differently. I think it's a matter of me being the only one willing to open my eyes and look at what is actually happening, while everyone else is either in on it or letting themselves be taken for a ride.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that others don’t see things the way you do. Even though they don’t agree with you, do you believe—without any doubt—that your interpretation of what Gary and the neighbors are doing is completely and absolutely true?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, doctor, absolutely without a single doubt. Like I was just trying to explain to you a moment ago, that is the entire reason I am sitting here in this chair today. Because for nearly two years now, I have been completely, one-hundred-percent sure that the contractor and some of those people on my block are quietly working together to cheat us. \n\nAnd they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! It is deliberate. You don't just 'accidentally' leave the plumbing for the dishwasher capped off for eighteen months so a sixty-seven-year-old woman has to haul her dirty plates down to the utility sink in the basement every single night. I get so tired going up and down those wooden stairs, Frank had to put down non-slip treads just last spring because I nearly took a tumble carrying a heavy stack of our good soup bowls. \n\nSo when you ask if I have any doubt... no, I do not. It does not matter one bit how many of those little faded thermal receipts my husband shows me from his green folder, trying to justify where the money went. And it doesn't matter how many excuses Gary and Linda give us about shipping delays from the manufacturer, or missing brackets, or whatever ridiculous story they've decided to spin for the week. I don't care about their excuses, and I don't care about Frank's paperwork. None of it changes the facts. I know exactly what they are doing to us, and my interpretation, as you call it, is the absolute truth. There is simply no other explanation, and I won't let anyone tell me otherwise.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that maybe your understanding of what’s happening with Gary and the neighbors could be wrong, even slightly?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like a broken record here, I really do. We just went over this, didn't we? No, I do not think I am wrong, not even a fraction of a percent. I mean, I am sitting here in this office for this exact reason, like I keep trying to make you understand. For nearly two years now, I have been absolutely certain that the contractor and those people on my street are quietly working together to cheat Frank and me. And they are doing it to keep my kitchen half-wrong on purpose! \n\nYou ask if I could be wrong, but how on earth could I be? I am not a foolish woman. I ran a cafeteria for three decades, I managed food orders, I know when inventory doesn't add up and I certainly know when people are pulling the wool over my eyes. Frank, bless him, he just doesn't want any conflict in his retirement. He will sit there in his brown leather recliner, pushing his reading glasses up his nose, and he will pull out yet another faded receipt from that green plastic folder of his and try to point out the dates for the lumber delivery or the plumbing fixtures. And then Gary will stand on my front porch with his little clipboard, wiping his forehead like he has actually been doing a hard day's work, and give me another endless string of excuses about the cabinet hardware being lost in transit or Linda needing him to look at a leak in her basement. \n\nBut it simply doesn't matter how many little slips of paper my husband shows me. And it doesn't matter how many elaborate excuses Gary and the neighbors give. I am not wrong. They are deliberately cheating us, and they are keeping my kitchen in a state of complete disaster on purpose. So no, doctor, I haven't ever thought my understanding of the situation was wrong, because the proof is right there in my house."
  }
